What is the Capital Disability benefit?
Capital Disability protects you against the risk of becoming
permanently unable to perform the duties of your chosen
or possibly a similar occupation. It provides you with a lump
sum to help cover your loss of earnings.
This is an accelerated benefit that can be added to either or
both of the Life Cover and Renewable Life Cover(1) benefits.
This benefit reduces the total of your Life Cover or
Renewable Life Cover(1) benefits by the amount of any
claim. Your other accelerated benefits may also be affected
by claiming since the maximum accelerated benefit cover
allowed is restricted to a maximum of the sum of the Life
Cover and Renewable Life Cover(1) benefits.

monthly benefit payments made under the post-retirement
phase of the benefit will not reduce Life Cover and
Renewable Life Cover(1) benefits, or any other accelerated
benefits.
What type of cover does the Capital Disability benefit
provide?
There are two types of cover that you can choose:
•

Note that when Capital Disability is selected as a term
benefit, only OOD will be available.

Additional cover provided by the Whole of Life term
After retirement you no longer face the risk of being unable
to perform the duties of your occupation, however you do
face the risk that you become permanently impaired and this
creates unforeseen expenses which effectively reduce your
income after retirement. If you have selected the Whole of
Life cover period option you will also be covered against this
risk in addition to the normal cover before retirement. If the
Whole of Life cover period has been chosen, and you become
permanently impaired (as defined in the policy document) after
retirement, the Capital Disability benefit will pay a monthly
benefit amount which increases each year by inflation (as
measured by CPI capped at 10.5%). The monthly payments
will continue for the remainder of your life. The amount of your
monthly benefit will be a proportion of your benefit amount
and depends on the severity of the impairment.
Even though Capital Disability is an accelerated benefit, any

Own occupation (OOD)
The benefit is payable if you cannot do your specific
job. For example if you are a surgeon and suffer from
an injury, then this benefit will be paid even if you have
the experience and training to become a lecturer.

•

Own or any reasonable occupation (OD)
The benefit will only be paid if you are unable to
perform your job or any reasonable job. So, using the
example above, if the surgeon has the experience and
training to be a lecturer, but is unable to perform this
role because of the disability, then the claim will be paid.

BENEFITS | LOSS OF INCOME PROTECTION PILLAR: LUMP SUM | OCTOBER 2025

LIFESTYLE PROTECTOR TECHNICAL GUIDE

BEFORE RETIREMENT

AFTER RETIREMENT

Your need:
Protection against the risk that you will not be able to earn an
income due to a disability.

Your need:
Protection against the risk that an impairment in old age leads to
expenses which effectively reduce your retirement income.

Conditions for payment:
You are unable to work due to a disability, as defined in the policy
document.

Conditions for payment:
You suffer an impairment after retirement, as defined, in the policy
document.

Type of payment:
A lump sum amount, which will reduce your Life Cover and/or
Renewable Life Cover(1).

Type of payment:
A monthly benefit payment, which increases by CPI each year
for the rest of your life. The monthly benefit payments will not
reduce your Life Cover or Renewable Life Cover (1) benefits. The
amount of the benefit depends on the severity of the impairment
suffered.

Before Retirement

Retirement

After I suffer from a disability or impairment after
retirement and claim, is there a waiting period before I
can receive my benefit?
No. Your claim will be processed as soon as Liberty has
established the permanence of the disability or impairment
after retirement as defined.
Are there any other factors that will affect my benefit?
Capital Disability is an accelerated benefit. The level of
cover selected for your Life Cover and Renewable Life
Cover(1) benefits and for example the Living Lifestyle
benefit (another accelerated benefit) may affect your
Capital Disability benefit. Claiming on an accelerated
benefit reduces your Life Cover. Your accelerated benefits
are restricted to a maximum of the total of the sums assured
of your Life Cover and Renewable Life Cover(1) benefits
at any stage of your policy. Therefore, claiming for Living
Lifestyle may reduce the amount that you may claim for
Capital Disability. Please see the section on Accelerated
Benefits versus Non-Accelerated Benefits for more details.

After Retirement

Death

If you have selected the Whole of Life cover period and a
post-retirement monthly claim is admitted, the monthly
claim payments will not reduce your Life Cover or
Renewable Life Cover(1), or any other accelerated benefits.
How does my occupation impact my benefit?
Your occupation has a direct bearing on your ability to claim
and hence your eventual benefit.
EXAMPLE

Consider Mr Jones, a bank manager, has the Capital
Disability (OOD) benefit. Mr Jones has an accident
and loses his dominant hand. While the injury would
impair his ability to play golf, Mr Jones would not be
able to claim for this injury if he could still perform his
occupational duties.
If Mr Jones were a surgeon, this injury would seriously
affect his ability to perform surgery in future and would
be a permanent condition. In this case, Liberty would
pay the claim.

BENEFITS | LOSS OF INCOME PROTECTION PILLAR: LUMP SUM | OCTOBER 2025

LIFESTYLE PROTECTOR TECHNICAL GUIDE

EXAMPLE

Mr Mashego has the Capital Disability (OOD) benefit
with the Whole of Life cover period and a sum assured
of R1 000 000 as well as Life Cover with a sum
assured of R1 000 000.
Claim before retirement
If Mr Mashego is in a car accident at age 35 (i.e. before
retirement) and is paralysed and, as a result, is unable
to perform his occupation, his Capital Disability
benefit will pay out R1 000 000. His Life Cover benefit
amount will reduce by R1 000 000 to zero and end.
Claim after retirement
If the car accident had occurred after retirement (at age
75 say), the claim would be assessed under the postretirement cover phase of the benefit. Paralysis qualifies
for a monthly payment of 1.5% of the benefit amount
and he will therefore be paid R15 000 per month
Whole of Life cover period: Why does the benefit
provide a lump sum before retirement, but a monthly
income benefit after retirement?
The Capital Disability benefit meets different needs before
and after retirement. Before retirement the benefit protects
you against the risk that you are unable to earn an income
due to a disability. Usually people will have some form of debt
before retirement, for example a house or car loan. A lump
sum payment can help settle that debt immediately should
you suffer a disability which prevents you from being able to
earn an income. After retirement, you no longer face the risk of
not being able to work but you do face the risk that you suffer
a serious impairment which leads to additional, unforeseen
expenses such as the cost of a nurse to care for you, or a driver
to transport you. These types of expenses will effectively
reduce your retirement income. Since you are not likely to
have any debt after retirement and your new expenses will be
incurred predominantly on a monthly basis, a monthly benefit
amount more closely meets your need for protection.
Can I select the Whole of Life cover period on both the
Capital Disability benefit and the Income Protector
benefit?
Yes, you may select the Whole of Life cover period on both
the Income Protector benefit and the Capital Disability
benefit, provided that:
•
•

1.5% of the Capital Disability sum assured; plus
50% of the Income Protector sum assured do not
exceed 55% of your after-tax insurable monthly income.

Whole of Life cover period: When will I be considered
retired for this benefit?
When assessing claims for this benefit you will be considered
retired if you are:
• At the policy anniversary immediately preceding your
70th birthday; or
• If you are over the age of 55 and you have stopped
active or full-time engagement in your occupation (but
not due to the impairment that you are claiming for).

(R 1 000 000 x 1.5%) and will be paid for the rest of
his life. These monthly benefit payments will help Mr
Mashego to meet the costs associated with being
paralysed in old age. For example, he may need to
employ a nurse on a full- or part-time basis to help care
for him.
These monthly payments will not reduce his Life
Cover benefit amount and his full R1 000 000 cover
will continue. In addition to the monthly benefit, Mr
Mashego will no longer have to pay the premiums for
the Capital Disability benefit. Mr Mashego’s benefit
payments will also increase annually in line with inflation.
Assuming inflation is 10%, his monthly claim payments
would increase to R16 500 in the second year of claim,
R18 150 in the third year and continue to increase on
every claim anniversary.
Whole of Life cover period: What will my monthly claim
amount be if I suffer an impairment after retirement?
Your claim amount will be equal to 0.5%, 1% or 1.5% of your
benefit amount depending on the type of impairment you
suffer and the severity of the impairment. Your benefit
amount will increase at each claim anniversary by inflation (as
measured by CPI and capped at 10.5% per annum).
More information on the levels of payout can be found in
the Impairment Benefit Categories section. However, you
should always consult your policy document for the claim
percentages applicable to your policy.
EXAMPLE

Mr Mashego has the Capital Disability benefit with a
Whole of Life cover period and a sum assured of
R1 000 000. At age 75 Mr Mashego suffers a brain
injury and starts to suffer from receptive aphasia and is
partially unable to comprehend language symbols. This
condition qualifies Mr Mashego for the 0.5% monthly
benefit level. This means that Mr Mashego will be paid
R5 000 per month (i.e. R1 000 000 x 0.5%) increasing
by inflation every year for the remainder of his life.
If Mr Mashego’s aphasia condition had been more
serious and he was totally unable to comprehend
language symbols he would have qualified for the
1.5% monthly benefit level. In this case his initial claim
amount would be R15 000 rather than the R5 000
described above.

BENEFITS | LOSS OF INCOME PROTECTION PILLAR: LUMP SUM | OCTOBER 2025

LIFESTYLE PROTECTOR TECHNICAL GUIDE

Whole of Life cover period: What is the Activities of
Daily Living (ADL) Catch-All Benefit Category?

Whole of Life cover period: What happens if I suffer a
second impairment or if my existing condition worsens?

The Activities of Daily Living (ADL) Catch-All Benefit
Category is one of the Impairment Benefit Categories
included in the impairment-based cover after retirement.
In this Benefit Category claims are assessed by considering
your ability to perform activities of daily life (known as ADLs)
rather than specific medical criteria. ADLs are made up of
“Basic ADLs” such as washing and dressing and “Instrumental
ADLs” such as telephone use and housekeeping. The aim
of this Benefit Category is to provide you with additional
security that should your impairment be serious enough to
cause you to be unable to perform a required number of
Basic and Instrumental ADLs, but is not listed in any of the
other benefit categories, your claim will still be assessed.
More information on the Activities of Daily Living (ADL)
Catch-All Benefit Category is included in the Impairment
Benefit Categories section. However, you should always
consult your policy document for the specific claim
percentages applicable to your policy document.

If you are already claiming for an impairment and your
condition worsens or if you suffer a different impairment and
either of these leads to you qualifying for a higher proportion
of the sum assured than you are already being paid, your
monthly benefit payment will be increased. The increase
will be calculated by taking your current monthly benefit
payment and multiplying it by the percentage increase in the
proportions of sum assured that are associated when moving
from the old to the new severity level.
EXAMPLE

Mr Smith has the Capital Disability benefit with
the Whole of Life cover period and a sum assured
of R1 000 000. Mr Smith retires at age 57. At age
64, Mr Smith suffers a heart attack and is diagnosed
with coronary artery disease. Since Mr Smith has
retired his claim will be assessed based on the after
retirement claims criteria. Suppose his condition
is at a severity level which qualifies him for a 0.5%
per month benefit. The monthly claim amount he
will receive is R5 000 per month (i.e. 0.5% of
R1 000 000).

Additional claims payment security:
Activities of Daily Living
Impairment Benefit
Categories with medicallybased claims criteria

If CPI was 10%, Mr Smith’s monthly benefit would
be increased by 10% to R5 500 after the first claim
anniversary and R6 050 after the second claim
anniversary.

Activities of Daily Living
(ADL) Catch-All Benefit
Category

Soon after the second claim anniversary Mr Smith
is diagnosed as suffering from severe aphasia and
meets the requirements for a payout level of 1.5%
which is higher than the current 0.5% his existing
claim amount is based on. Since 1.5% is 3 times
0.5% his benefit will be increased by a factor of 3.
This means that his new benefit amount will
be R18 150 (i.e. 3 x R6 050). Note how the inflation
increases that Mr Smith has already received are
also increased when the severity level claimed on
increases.

BENEFITS | LOSS OF INCOME PROTECTION PILLAR: LUMP SUM | OCTOBER 2025

LIFESTYLE PROTECTOR TECHNICAL GUIDE

What type of cover does the Capital Disability Plus
benefit provide?

What is the difference between Capital Disability Plus
and Capital Disability?

There are two types of cover that you can choose:
• Own occupation (OOD)
The benefit is payable if you cannot do your specific job.
For example, if you are a surgeon and suffer from an
injury, then this benefit will be paid even if you have the
experience and training to become a lecturer.
Note that when Capital Disability Plus is selected as a
term benefit, only OOD will be available.
• Own or any reasonable occupation (OD)
The benefit will only be paid if you are unable to perform
your job or any reasonable job. So, using the example
above, if the surgeon has the experience and training to
be a lecturer, but is unable to perform this role because
of the disability, then the claim will be paid.

Capital Disability Plus is a non-accelerated benefit.
This means that claiming on this benefit will not affect any
of your other benefits.
Capital Disability is an accelerated benefit. Claiming for
this benefit will reduce the total of your Life Cover and
Renewable Life Cover(2) by the amount of the claim under
this benefit (and may also reduce your Living Lifestyle if
selected). The operation of the underlying benefit does not
differ between the two benefits. It is the effect of claiming on
your other benefits that differs.
The Capital Disability Plus benefit is more comprehensive
than Capital Disability and hence more expensive, as
explained below:

After I suffer from a disability or impairment, after
retirement and claim, is there a waiting period before
I can receive my benefit?

EXAMPLE

Consider Mr Jones who takes out R1 000 000 Life
Cover and R750 000 own occupation Capital Disability
Plus. At age 35 Mr Jones has an accident and cannot
perform the duties of his job any more. Mr Jones claims
his Capital Disability Plus benefit for R750 000.

No. Your claim will be paid as soon as Liberty has established
the permanence of the disability or impairment (after
retirement).
How does my occupation impact my benefit?

Some years on Mr Jones passes away. His beneficiaries
then claim his Life Cover benefit for R1 000 000.

Your occupation has a direct bearing on your ability to claim
and hence your eventual benefit.

If Mr Jones had instead selected R1 000 000 Life Cover
and R750 000 own occupation Capital Disability
(the accelerated benefit) and suffered an occupational
disability, he is still entitled to his Capital Disability
benefit of R750 000. However, when Mr Jones passes
away, his beneficiaries are only able to claim R250 000.
His Life Cover of R1 000 000 was accelerated when he
made his disability claim and was reduced by the amount
of the claim (R1 000 000 - R750 000 = R250 000).

EXAMPLE

Consider Mr Jones, a bank manager, who has the Capital
Disability Plus (OOD) benefit. Mr Jones had an accident
and loses his dominant hand. While the injury would
impair his ability to play golf, Mr Jones would not be
able to claim for this injury if he could still perform his
occupational duties.
If Mr Jones were a surgeon, this injury would seriously
affect his ability to perform surgery in future and would
be a permanent condition. In this case, Liberty would pay
the claim.
Whole of Life cover period: Why does the benefit
provide a lump sum before retirement, but a monthly
income benefit after retirement?
The Capital Disability Plus benefit meets different needs
before and after retirement. Before retirement the benefit
protects you against the risk that you are unable to earn an
income due to a disability. Usually people will have some
form of debt before retirement, for example a house or
car loan. A lump sum payment can help settle that debt
immediately should you suffer a disability which prevents
you from being able to earn an income. After retirement,
you no longer face the risk of not being able to work but
you do face the risk that you suffer a serious impairment
which leads to additional, unforeseen expenses such as
the cost of a nurse to care for you, or a driver to transport
you. These types of expenses will effectively reduce your
retirement income. Since you are not likely to have any debt
after retirement and that your new expenses will be incurred
predominantly on a monthly basis, a monthly benefit
amount more closely meets your need for protection.

Can I select the Whole of Life cover period on both the
Capital Disability Plus benefit and the Income Protector
benefit?
Yes, you may select the Whole of Life cover period on both
the Income Protector benefit and the Capital Disability
Plus benefit, provided that:
•

1.5% of the Capital Disability Plus sum assured; plus

• 50% of the Income Protector sum assured
do not exceed 55% of your after-tax insurable monthly
income.

Whole of Life cover period: When will I be
considered retired for this benefit?
When assessing claims for this benefit you will be considered
retired:
•

At the policy anniversary immediately preceding your
70th birthday; or

•

If you are over the age of 55 and you have stopped
active or full-time engagement in your occupation (but
not due to the impairment that you are claiming for).

BENEFITS | LOSS OF INCOME PROTECTION PILLAR: LUMP SUM | OCTOBER 2025

LIFESTYLE PROTECTOR TECHNICAL GUIDE

Whole of Life cover period: What will my monthly claim
amount be if I suffer an impairment after retirement?

Whole of Life cover period: What is the Activities of
Daily Living (ADL) Catch-All Benefit Category?

Your claim amount will be equal to 0.5%, 1% or 1.5% of your
benefit amount depending on the type of impairment you
suffer and the severity of the impairment. Your benefit
amount will increase at each claim anniversary by inflation (as
measured by CPI and capped at 10.5% Per annum).

The Activities of Daily Living (ADL) Catch-All Benefit
Category is one of the Impairment Benefit Categories
included in the impairment-based cover after retirement.
In this Benefit Category claims are assessed by considering
your ability to perform activities of daily life (known as ADLs)
rather than specific medical criteria. ADLs are made up of
“Basic ADLs” such as washing and dressing and “Instrumental
ADLs” such as telephone use and housekeeping.

More information on the levels of payout, can be found in
the Impairment Benefit Categories section. However, you
should always consult your policy document for the claim
percentages applicable to your policy.
EXAMPLE

Mr Mashego has the Capital Disability Plus benefit
with a Whole of Life cover period and a sum assured of
R1 000 000. At age 75 Mr Mashego suffers a brain
injury and starts to suffer from receptive aphasia and is
partially unable to comprehend language symbols.
This condition qualifies Mr Mashego for the 0.5%
monthly benefit level. This means that Mr Mashego will
be paid R5 000 per month (i.e. R1 000 000 x 0.5%)
increasing by inflation every year for the remainder of
his life.
If Mr Mashego’s aphasia condition had been more
serious and he was totally unable to comprehend
language symbols he would have qualified for the
1.5% monthly benefit level. In this case his initial claim
amount would be R15 000 rather than the R5 000
described above.

The aim of this Benefit Category is to provide you with
additional security that should your impairment be serious
enough to cause you to be unable to perform a required
number of Basic and Instrumental ADLs, but is not listed
in any of the other benefit categories, your claim will still
be assessed. More information on the Activities of Daily
Living (ADL) Catch-All Benefit Category is included in the
Impairment Benefit Categories section. However, you should
always consult your policy document for the specific claim
percentages applicable to your policy document.

BENEFITS | LOSS OF INCOME PROTECTION PILLAR: LUMP SUM | OCTOBER 2025

Whole of Life cover period: What happens if I suffer a
second impairment or if my existing condition worsens?
If you are already claiming for an impairment and your
condition worsens or if you suffer a different impairment and
either of these leads to you qualifying for a higher proportion

LIFESTYLE PROTECTOR TECHNICAL GUIDE

of the sum assured than you are already being paid, your
monthly benefit payment will be increased. The increase
will be calculated by taking your current monthly benefit
payment and multiplying it by the percentage increase in the
proportions of sum assured that are associated when moving
from the old to the new severity level.

EXAMPLE

Mr Smith has the Capital Disability Plus benefit with the
Whole of Life cover period and a sum assured of
R1 000 000. Mr Smith retires at age 57. At age 64,
Mr Smith suffers a heart attack and is diagnosed with
coronary artery disease. Since Mr Smith has retired his
claim will be assessed based on the after retirement
claims criteria. Suppose his condition is at a severity
level which qualifies him for a 0.5% per month benefit.
The monthly claim amount he will receive is R5 000 per
month (i.e. 0.5% of R1 000 000).
If CPI was 10%, Mr Smith’s monthly benefit would be
increased by 10% to R5 500 after the first claim

anniversary and R6 050 after the second claim
anniversary.
Soon after the second claim anniversary Mr Smith is
diagnosed as suffering from severe aphasia and meets
the requirements for a payout level of 1.5% which is
higher than the current 0.5% his existing claim amount
is based on. Since 1.5% is 3 times 0.5% his benefit will
be increased by a factor of 3. This means that his new
benefit amount will be R18 150 (i.e. 3 x R6 050). Note
how the inflation increases that Mr Smith has already
received are also increased when the severity level
claimed on increases.

IMPAIRMENT
Benefit Description

Impairment pays you a lump sum if you are permanently impaired. The size of the payment depends
on the severity of your impairment.
If you have selected the Whole of Life cover period, once you are retired the nature of your cover will
change. After retirement, if you become permanently impaired as defined in the policy document,
you will receive a monthly benefit which increases each year with inflation (as measured by CPI
capped at 10.5%) for the remainder of your life. The amount of your monthly benefit is a proportion of
the sum assured and depends on the severity of your impairment.

Benefit Type

This is an accelerated benefit that can be added to either or both of the Life Cover and the
Renewable Life Cover(1) benefits on the policy. If you have selected the To end of the month of the
Life Assured’s: 65th or70th birthday cover period, or if you have selected the Whole of Life cover
period and claim before retirement, this benefit reduces the total of your Life Cover and Renewable
Life Cover(1) by the amount of any claim. Your other accelerated benefits may also be affected by
claiming since the maximum accelerated benefit cover allowed is restricted to the amount of your
Life Cover plus your Renewable Life Cover(1).
If you have selected the Whole of Life cover period, any claims for this benefit after retirement will not
reduce your Life Cover or Renewable Life Cover(1) even though it is an accelerated benefit.

Cover Period

• Whole of Life
• To end of the month of the Life Assured’s: 65th or 70th birthday

Benefit Limits

Minimum: R100 000
Maximum: 	R60 million (To end of the month of the Life Assured’s: 65th or 70th birthday cover
periods), R9 million (Whole of Life cover period), aggregated over all Loss of Income
Protection benefits If the Whole of Life cover period is selected on both the Income
Protector benefit and Impairment benefit, the sum of 1.5% of the Impairment sum
assured and 50% of the Income Protector sum assured cannot exceed 55% of after-tax
insurable monthly income.

BENEFITS | LOSS OF INCOME PROTECTION PILLAR: LUMP SUM | OCTOBER 2025

LIFESTYLE PROTECTOR TECHNICAL GUIDE

What is the Impairment benefit?

Additional cover provided by the Whole of Life term

Impairment provides you with a lump sum to cover
the loss of your ability to earn income should you suffer
from an impairment that is covered under this benefit.
Impairment refers to the loss of function in any part of the
body, whether it is physical or mental.

After retirement you no longer face the risk of being unable
to work if you become permanently impaired, however you
do face the risk that you become permanently impaired and
this creates unforeseen expenses which effectively reduce
your income after retirement. If you have selected the Whole
of Life cover period option you will also be covered against
this risk in addition to the normal cover before retirement.
If the Whole of Life cover period has been chosen, and you
become permanently impaired (as defined in the policy
document) after retirement, the Impairment benefit will
pay a monthly benefit amount which increases each year
by inflation (as measured by CPI capped at 10.5%). The
monthly payments will continue for the remainder of your
life. The amount of your monthly benefit will be a proportion
of your benefit amount and depends on the severity of the
impairment.

This is an accelerated benefit that can be added to either
or both of Life Cover and Renewable Life Cover(1).
This benefit reduces the total of your Life Cover and
Renewable Life Cover(1) benefit by the amount of any
claim. Your other accelerated benefits may also be affected
by claiming since the maximum accelerated benefit cover
allowed is restricted to the amount of your Life Cover
benefit plus your Renewable Life Cover(1) benefit.
EXAMPLE

Mr Jones has a Lifestyle Protector policy with a
R 1 000 000 Life Cover benefit and a R 500 000
Impairment benefit. Mr Jones is involved in a motor
vehicle accident and loses his leg below the knee. He is
regarded as permanently impaired. In accordance with
the benefit criteria under The Limbs benefit category, Mr
Jones’ claim is valid for a 50% pay out of his Impairment
cover amount. Mr Jones is paid out R 250 000, which
reduces his Impairment cover amount to R 250 000. In
addition, his Life Cover benefit is also reduced by the pay
out and is now valued at R 750 000. Any subsequent pay
outs under the Impairment benefit will further reduce the
Life Cover benefit cover amount.

Even though Impairment is an accelerated benefit, any
monthly benefit payments made under the post-retirement
phase of the benefit will not reduce Life Cover and
Renewable Life Cover(1) benefits.

BEFORE RETIREMENT

Your need:
Protection against the risk that you will not be able to earn an
income due to an impairment.

Your need:
Protection against the risk that an impairment in old age leads to
expenses which effectively reduce your retirement income.

Conditions for payment:
You suffer an impairment before retirement, as defined in the
policy document.

Conditions for payment:
You suffer an impairment after retirement, as defined, in the policy
document.

Type of payment:
A lump sum amount which will
reduce your Life Cover and/or
Renewable Life Cover(1). The lump
sum amount depends on the severity
of the impairment suffered.

Type of payment:
A monthly benefit payment, which increases by CPI each year
for the rest of your life. The monthly benefit payment will not
reduce your Life Cover or Renewable Life Coverr(1) benefits. The
amount of the benefit depends on the severity of the impairment
suffered.

EXAMPLE

Mr Mashego has the Impairment benefit with the
Whole of Life cover period and a sum assured of
R1 000 000 as well as Life Cover with a sum assured
of R1 000 000.
Claim before retirement
If Mr Mashego is in a car accident at age 35 (i.e.
before retirement) and is permanently paralysed. His
Impairment benefit will pay out R1 000 000, his Life
Cover benefit amount will reduce by R1 000 000 to zero
and end.
Claim after retirement
If the same motor vehicle accident had occurred when
Mr Mashego turned 75, the claim would be assessed
under the post-retirement benefits. Paralysis qualifies
for a monthly payment of 1.5% of the benefit amount.
Mr Mashego will thus receive R15 000 per month
(R1 000 000 x 1.5%), which will be paid for the rest of
his life.
These monthly benefit payments will help Mr Mashego
to meet the costs associated with being paralysed in
old age. For example, he may need to employ a nurse
on a full- or part-time basis to help care for him. These
monthly payments will not reduce his Life Cover benefit
amount and his full R1 000 000 cover will continue.
In addition to the monthly benefit, Mr Mashego will no
longer have to pay the premiums for the Impairment
benefit. Mr Mashego’s benefit payments will also
increase annually in line with inflation. Assuming inflation
is 10%, his monthly claim payments would increase
to R16 500 in the second year of claim, R18 150 in the
third year, and continue to increase every claim year
thereafter.

LIFESTYLE PROTECTOR TECHNICAL GUIDE

What type of cover does the Impairment benefit
provide?
Impairment is intended to protect you against potential
loss of income. Payment is dependent on the level
of impairment suffered rather than whether you
are occupationally disabled. Impairment provides a
comprehensive benefit. The benefit payment is proportional
to the severity of your condition.
EXAMPLE

If Mr Jones becomes legally blind in one eye at age
30, he will receive 25% of his sum assured. However,
if Mr Jones becomes legally blind in both eyes, he will
receive 100% of his sum assured.

EXAMPLE

Mr Jones purchased a Lifestyle Protector policy with a
Life Cover benefit of R 1 000 000. To this, he adds an
Impairment benefit with a cover amount of
R 1 000 000.
At age 30, Mr Jones loses his thumb through the MP
joint on his left hand. Mr Jones can claim 25% of his
sum assured, under the Limbs benefit category, for a
permanent impairment. He is paid R250 000 of his
R1 000 000 Impairment benefit cover amount. After
the claim pay out, Mr Jones new cover amounts are
R750 000 for both the Life Cover and Impairment
benefits. Mr Jones can expect to pay an adjusted
premium, which will be lower and considers the reduced
cover amounts.
A year later Mr Jones loses his left arm below the elbow
in a car accident, again rendering him permanently
impaired. He is eligible for a claim of 50% of his cover
amount under the Limbs benefit category. However, he
has already been paid 25% under this benefit category.
As a result of this, Mr Jones will qualify for a further 25%
of his cover amount. This will bring the total pay-out
under the benefit category to 50%. He is paid R187 500
(25% of his remaining sum assured of R750 000).
His Life Cover and Impairment sums assured are
recalculated and reduced to R562 500.

BENEFITS | LOSS OF INCOME PROTECTION PILLAR: LUMP SUM | OCTOBER 2025

LIFESTYLE PROTECTOR TECHNICAL GUIDE

How do benefit increases affect the tiered claim
payments?

What is the Activities of Daily Living (ADL)
Catch-All Benefit Category?

The amount of any claim is based on the sum assured at the
date of claim and the percentage of the sum assured payable
as a result of the impairment.

The Activities of Daily Living (ADL) Catch-All Benefit
Category is one of the Impairment Benefit Categories. In
this Benefit Category claims are assessed by considering
your ability to perform activities of daily life (known
as ADLs) rather than medical criteria. ADLs are made
up of “Basic ADLs” such as washing and dressing
and “Instrumental ADLs” such as telephone use and
housekeeping. The aim of this Benefit Category is to
provide you with additional security that should your
impairment be serious enough to cause you to be unable
to perform a required number of Basic and Instrumental
ADLs, but is not listed in any of the other benefit
categories, your claim will still be assessed.
More information on the Activities of Daily Living (ADL)
Catch-All Benefit Category is included in the Impairment
Benefit Categories section. However, you should always
consult your policy document for the specific claim
percentages applicable to your policy document.

EXAMPLE

Mr Smith has the Life Cover and Impairment benefits
with a R 1 000 000 cover amount for each and a 7%
annual benefit increase. In the first year, Mr Smith loses
his hand at the MP joint. He is permanently impaired,
and his claim qualifies for a 50% pay out amounting to
R 500 000. This reduces both his benefits to R 500 000.
At anniversary of his policy, his cover amounts increase
by 7% to R 535 000.
Within the second year of his policy, Mr Smith is involved
in a motor vehicle accident and is rendered paraplegic.
Paraplegia qualifies for a 100% pay-out under the
Impairment benefit, and Mr Smith receives the full value
of the remaining cover amount of R 535 000. Once the
claim is paid out, both the Life Cover and Impairment
benefits will cease.

Additional claims payment security:
Activities of Daily Living
Impairment Benefit
Categories with medicallybased claims criteria

Activities of Daily Living
(ADL) Catch-All Benefit
Category

EXAMPLE

Mr Smith contracts a very rare and unusual disease at
age 35. The disease is not included explicitly in the list
of impairments covered by his Impairment benefit.
It is, however, severely, and permanently impacting
Mr Smith’s ability to lead a normal life. Mr Smith’s
impairment will be assessed under the Activities of
Daily Living (ADL) Catch-All Benefit Category.
His claim will be assessed based on the number of Basic
and Instrumental ADLs he cannot perform. If Mr Smith
is permanently unable to perform 1 Basic ADL and 2
Instrumental ADLs, he will be paid 50% of his sum
assured. If Mr Smith is unable to perform 3 Basic ADLs
and 4 instrumental ADLs, he would be paid his full sum
assured.
After I suffer from an impairment and claim, is there a
waiting period before I can receive my benefit?
No. Your claim will be paid as soon as Liberty has established
the permanence of the condition you are claiming for.

BENEFITS | LOSS OF INCOME PROTECTION PILLAR: LUMP SUM | OCTOBER 2025

LIFESTYLE PROTECTOR TECHNICAL GUIDE

What is the difference between the Impairment benefit
and the Capital Disability benefit?

Can I select the Whole of Life cover period on both the
Impairment benefit and the Income Protector benefit?

The Impairment benefit provides a benefit when an injury
or disease affects your functionality. Capital Disability
provides a benefit when the injury or disease affects your
ability to perform the duties of your occupation. While both
benefits provide comprehensive cover to replace a loss of
earnings, each has its own claims criteria. Depending on your
needs, one benefit might be more suitable than the other
(see the example below).

Yes, you may select the Whole of Life cover period on both
the Income Protector benefit and the Impairment benefit,
provided that:
• 1.5% of the Impairment sum assured; plus

The Capital Disability benefit and the Impairment benefit
provide the same cover after retirement if the Whole of Life
cover periods have been selected for the benefits.
EXAMPLE

The Impairment benefit will pay a claim based on
the level of physical, functional or mental impairment
suffered. The Capital Disability benefit will pay a
claim assessed on your ability to do the duties of your
occupation.
A neurosurgeon’s ability to do his job would be
seriously affected if he lost his hand. He would be paid
more under the Capital Disability benefit with own
occupation cover than under the Impairment benefit.
A bank manager who lost his hand would not be
occupationally disabled and would receive no payment
if he had the Capital Disability benefit. He would,
however, receive payment under the Impairment
benefit.
Depending on your occupation and the claim event, the
Capital Disability benefit or the Impairment benefit
might better suit your needs.
Whole of Life cover period: Why does the benefit
provide a lump sum before retirement, but a monthly
income benefit after retirement?
The Impairment benefit meets different needs before and
after retirement. Before retirement the benefit protects you
against the risk that you are unable to earn an income due
to an impairment. Usually people will have some form of
debt before retirement, for example a house or car loan. A
lump sum payment can help settle that debt immediately
should you suffer an impairment which prevents you from
being able to earn an income. After retirement, you no
longer face the risk of not being able to work but you do face
the risk that you suffer a serious impairment which leads to
additional, unforeseen expenses such as the cost of a nurse
to care for you, or a driver to transport you. These types of
expenses will effectively reduce your retirement income.
Since you are not likely to have any debt after retirement
and that your new expenses will be incurred predominantly
on a monthly basis, a monthly benefit amount more closely
meets your need for protection.

•

50% of the Income Protector sum assured do not
exceed 55% of your after-tax insurable monthly income.

Whole of Life cover period: When will I be considered
retired for this benefit?
When assessing claims for this benefit you will be considered
retired:
•

At the policy anniversary immediately preceding your
70th birthday; or

•

If you are over the age of 55 and you have stopped
active or full-time engagement in your occupation (but
not due to the impairment that you are claiming for).

Whole of Life cover period: What will my monthly claim
amount be if I suffer an impairment after retirement?
Your claim amount will be equal to 0.5%, 1% or 1.5% of your
benefit amount depending on the type of impairment you
suffer and the severity of the impairment. Your benefit
amount will increase at each claim anniversary by inflation (as
measured by CPI and capped at 10.5% per annum).
More information on the levels of payout can be found in
the Impairment Benefit Categories section. However, you
should always consult your policy document for the claim
percentages applicable to your policy.
EXAMPLE

Mr Mashego has the Impairment benefit with a Whole
of Life cover period and a sum assured of R1 000 000.
At age 75 (post-retirement), Mr Mashego suffers a brain
injury and starts to suffer from permanent receptive
aphasia and is partially unable to comprehend language
symbols. This condition qualifies Mr Mashego for
the 0.5% monthly benefit level. This means that Mr
Mashego will be paid R5 000 per month (i.e., R1 000
000 x 0.5%) increasing by inflation every year for the
remainder of his life.
If Mr Mashego’s aphasia condition had been more
serious and he was totally unable to comprehend
language symbols he would have qualified for the
1.5% monthly benefit level. In this case his initial claim
amount would be R15 000 per month rather than the
R5 000 per month described above.

BENEFITS | LOSS OF INCOME PROTECTION PILLAR: LUMP SUM | OCTOBER 2025

Whole of Life cover period: What happens if I suffer an
impairment and then either my condition worsens or I
suffer a second impairment?
If you have already made a partial claim on your benefit, you
will still receive an additional claim payment (or an increased
claim payment) if either your existing impairment worsens or
if you suffer another unrelated impairment.
A more detailed explanation of the treatment of multiple
claims is included in the Impairment Benefit categories
section.
EXAMPLE

Mr Smith has the Impairment benefit with the Whole
of Life cover period and a sum assured of R1 000 000.
At age 34 Mr Smith is involved in an accident at work
and loses his foot. This qualifies for a 25% payment and
he is paid R250 000 (25% of R1 000 000). His sum
assured reduces to R750 000.
At age 35, Mr Smith is in a car accident and loses his
left arm below his elbow. This qualifies him for a 50%
payment under the “Limbs” Benefit Category but
since he has already been paid 25% for that Benefit
Category he will receive 25% of his sum assured.
Therefore he will be paid R187 500 (i.e. 25% of R 750
000). His sum assured reduces to R 562 500.
Suppose that many years later, at age 74, Mr Smith
suffers a heart attack and is diagnosed with coronary
artery disease. Since Mr Smith is considered retired
he will be assessed under the post-retirement phase
of the benefit. Suppose the severity of his condition
qualifies him for
0.5% of his sum assured as a monthly benefit amount.
He will be paid R2 813 per month (i.e. 0.5% of R562
500). As a post-retirement claim has been admitted,
premiums are no longer required to be paid for the
benefit.
After every claim anniversary Mr Smith’s claim
amount will be increased by CPI (capped at 10.5%).
If CPI is 10% his claim amount will be increased to R3
094 at the first claim anniversary, R3 403 after the
second claim anniversary and the annual escalation
will continue to be applied at every claim anniversary
thereafter.
Soon after the second claim anniversary Mr Smith is
diagnosed as suffering from severe aphasia and meets
the criteria for the 1.5% per month payout level. Mr
Smith’s benefit of R3 403 will be increased by a factor
of 3, since the 1.5% payout level he qualifies for is 3
times the 0.5% payout level he is currently receiving.
Mr Smith’s monthly claim amount therefore increases
to R10 209.
(1)

BENEFITS | LOSS OF INCOME PROTECTION PILLAR: LUMP SUM | OCTOBER 2025

What is the Impairment Plus benefit?
Impairment Plus provides you with a lump sum to cover the
loss of your ability to earn an income, should you suffer from
an impairment that is covered under this benefit. Impairment
refers to the loss of function in any part of the body, whether
it is physical, functional or mental.
EXAMPLE

Mr Jones starts to suffer from severe, permanent
hearing loss. If Mr Jones’s binaural hearing loss is
between 70 and 87 decibels the Impairment Plus
benefit will pay out 50% of his sum assured. If Mr
Jones’s binaural hearing loss is greater than 87 decibels
his Impairment Plus benefit will pay out 100% of his
sum assured.
Additional cover provided by the Whole of Life term
After retirement you no longer face the risk of being unable
to work if you become permanently impaired, however you
do face the risk that you become permanently impaired and
this creates unforeseen expenses which effectively reduce
your income after retirement. If you have selected the Whole
of Life Cover period option you will also be covered against
this risk in addition to the normal cover before retirement.
If the Whole of Life cover period has been chosen, and you
become permanently impaired (as defined in the policy
document) after retirement, the Impairment Plus benefit
will pay a monthly benefit amount which increases each
year by inflation (as measured by CPI capped at 10.5%). The
monthly payments will continue for the remainder of your
life. The amount of your monthly benefit will be a proportion
of your benefit amount and depends on the severity of the
impairment.

LIFESTYLE PROTECTOR TECHNICAL GUIDE

EXAMPLE

Mr Mashego has the Impairment Plus benefit with a
sum assured of R 1 000 000. In addition, he also has a
Life Cover benefit with a cover amount of R 1 000 000.
Claim before retirement
Mr Mashego is in a motor vehicle accident at age 35
(i.e., before retirement) and is permanently paralysed.
His claim will qualify for a 100% pay out under the
Impairment Plus benefit and he will receive R 1 000 000.
This pay-out will not reduce his Life Cover benefit cover
amount.
Claim after retirement
If the same motor vehicle accident had occurred when
Mr Mashego turned 75, the claim would be assessed
under the post-retirement benefits.
Paralysis qualifies for a monthly pay out of 1.5% of the
cover amount. Mr Mashego will receive R 15 000 per
month (R 1 000 000 x 1.5%), which will be paid out for
the rest of his life. The monthly benefit pay-outs will
help Mr Mashego to meet the costs associated with
being paralysed in old age. An example of this would be
to employ a nurse on a full or part time basis to care for
him.
The monthly pay-outs will not reduce his Life Cover
benefit. In addition, Mr Mashego will not be required to
continue premium payments for his Impairment Plus
benefit. His benefit pay-outs will increase annually in line
with inflation. Assuming that inflation is 10%, his monthly
claim pay-outs would increase to R 16 500 in the second
year of claim, R 18 150 in the third year and continue to
increase every claim year thereafter.

What type of cover does the Impairment Plus benefit
provide?
Impairment Plus is intended to protect you against
potential loss of income. Payment is dependent on the
level of impairment suffered rather than whether you are
occupationally disabled. Impairment Plus provides a
comprehensive benefit. The benefit payment is proportional
to the severity of your condition.
EXAMPLE

If Mr Jones becomes legally blind in one eye at age 30,
he will receive 25% of his sum assured. However, if Mr
Jones becomes legally blind in both eyes, he will receive
100% of his sum assured.

BENEFITS | LOSS OF INCOME PROTECTION PILLAR: LUMP SUM | OCTOBER 2025

How do benefit increases affect the tiered claim
payments?
The amount of any claim is based on the sum assured at the
date of claim, the percentage of the sum assured payable as
a result of the impairment, and any impairment claims that
have already been paid.

LIFESTYLE PROTECTOR TECHNICAL GUIDE

Additional claims payment security:
Activities of Daily Living

Impairment Benefit
Categories with medicallybased claims criteria

EXAMPLE

Mr Smith has the Impairment Plus benefit with a
R1 000 000 sum assured and a 7% automatic benefit
increase. After 10 months Mr Smith loses his hand and
submits a claim.
This renders him permanently functionally impaired,
and a 50% benefit ( R700 000) is paid, under The
Limbs benefit category. At the first policy anniversary
the sum assured increases by 7% to R1 070 000.
Mr Smith submits a claim 18 months after inception of
the policy after he was involved in a car accident where
he suffers a spinal injury that leaves him permanently
impaired as per the benefit criteria. 100% of the benefit
is due, however 50% has already been paid of the
original sum assured. Now, the increased sum assured
is used to calculate the value of the claim pay-out.
Therefore, the remaining portion of the sum assured
(R1 070 000 x 50% = R535 000) is paid and the
benefit ceases.

What is the Activities of Daily Living (ADL) Catch-All
Benefit Category?
The Activities of Daily Living (ADL) Catch-All Benefit
Category is one of the Impairment Benefit Categories. In
this Benefit Category claims are assessed by considering
your ability to perform activities of daily life (known as ADLs)
rather than medical criteria. ADLs are made up of “Basic
ADLs” such as washing and dressing and “Instrumental ADLs”
such as telephone use and housekeeping. The aim of this
Benefit Category is to provide you with additional security
that should your impairment be serious enough to cause
you to be unable to perform a required number of Basic
and Instrumental ADLs, but is not listed in any of the other
benefit categories, your claim will still be assessed. More
information on the Activities of Daily Living (ADL) CatchAll Benefit Category is included in the Impairment Benefit
Categories section. However, you should always consult
your policy document for the specific claim percentages
applicable to your policy document.

Activities of Daily Living
(ADL) Catch-All Benefit
Category

EXAMPLE

Mr Smith contracts a very rare and unusual disease at
age 35. The disease is not included explicitly in the list of
impairments covered by his Impairment Plus benefit.
It is however, severely, and permanently impacting
Mr Smith’s ability to lead a normal life. Mr Smith’s
impairment will be assessed under the Activities of
Daily Living (ADL) Catch-All Benefit Category. His claim
will be assessed based on the number of Basic and
Instrumental ADLs he cannot perform. If Mr Smith
is permanently unable to perform 1 Basic ADL and
2 Instrumental ADLs he will be paid 50% of his sum
assured. If Mr Smith is unable to perform 3 Basic ADLs
or 4 Instrumental ADLs he would be paid his full sum
assured.

After I suffer from an impairment and claim, is there a
waiting period before I can receive my benefit?
No. Your claim will be paid as soon as Liberty has established
the permanence of the condition you are claiming for.

BENEFITS | LOSS OF INCOME PROTECTION PILLAR: LUMP SUM | OCTOBER 2025

What is the difference between the Impairment Plus
benefit and the Capital Disability Plus benefit?
Impairment Plus provides a benefit when an injury or
disease affects your functionality. Capital Disability Plus
provides a benefit when the injury or disease affects your
ability to perform the duties of your occupation. While both
benefits provide comprehensive cover to replace a loss of
earnings, each has its own claims criteria. Depending on your
needs, one benefit might be more suitable than the other
(see the example below).
The Capital Disability Plus benefit and the Impairment
Plus benefit provide the same cover after retirement if the
Whole of Life cover periods have been selected for the
benefits. Whilst there are advantages and disadvantages
to both these benefits, Liberty recognises that individuals
may require either benefit or some combination of the two
benefits. Anticipating this need, Liberty offers a combination
benefit called Absolute Protector Plus (discussed under
the relevant section).
EXAMPLE

LIFESTYLE PROTECTOR TECHNICAL GUIDE

Whole of Life cover period: Why does the benefit
provide a lump sum before retirement, but a monthly
income benefit after retirement?
The Impairment Plus benefit meets different needs before
and after retirement. Before retirement the benefit protects
you against the risk that you are unable to earn an income
due to an impairment. Usually people will have some form
of debt before retirement, for example a house or car loan.
A lump sum payment can help settle that debt immediately
should you suffer an impairment which prevents you from
being able to earn an income. After retirement, you no
longer face the risk of not being able to work but you do face
the risk that you suffer a serious impairment which leads to
additional, unforeseen expenses such as the cost of a nurse
to care for you, or a driver to transport you. These types of
expenses will effectively reduce your retirement income.
Since you are not likely to have any debt after retirement
and that your new expenses will be incurred predominantly
on a monthly basis, a monthly benefit amount more closely
meets your need for protection.
Can I select the Whole of Life cover period on both the
Impairment benefit and the Income Protector benefit?

Impairment Plus will pay a claim based on the level
of physical, functional or mental Impairment suffered.
Capital Disability Plus will pay a claim assessed on
your ability to do the duties of your occupation.

Yes, you may select the Whole of Life cover period on both
the Income Protector benefit and the Impairment benefit,
provided that:
•

1.5% of the Impairment sum assured; plus

A neurosurgeon’s ability to do his job would be seriously
affected if he lost his hand. He would be paid more
under Capital Disability Plus with own occupation
cover than under Impairment Plus.

•

50% of the Income Protector sum assured

A bank manager who lost his hand would not be
occupationally disabled and would receive no payment
if he had Capital Disability Plus. He would however,
receive payment under Impairment Plus.
Depending on your occupation and the claim event,
Capital Disability Plus or Impairment Plus might
better suit your needs.

do not exceed 55% of your after-tax insurable monthly
income.

BENEFITS | LOSS OF INCOME PROTECTION PILLAR: LUMP SUM | OCTOBER 2025

LIFESTYLE PROTECTOR TECHNICAL GUIDE

Whole of Life cover period: When will I be considered
retired for this benefit?

A more detailed explanation of the treatment of multiple claims
is included in the Impairment Benefit Categories section.

When assessing claims for this benefit you will be considered
retired:

EXAMPLE

•

At the policy anniversary immediately preceding your
70th birthday; or

•

If you are over the age of 55 and you have stopped
active or full-time engagement in your occupation (but
not due to the impairment that you are claiming for).

Whole of Life cover period: What will my monthly claim
amount be if I suffer an impairment after retirement?
Your claim amount will be equal to 0.5%, 1% or 1.5% of your
benefit amount depending on the type of impairment you
suffer and the severity of the impairment. Your benefit
amount will increase at each claim anniversary by inflation (as
measured by CPI and capped at 10.5% per annum).
More information on the levels of payout, can be found in
the Impairment Benefit Categories section. However, you
should always consult your policy document for the claim
percentages applicable to your policy.
EXAMPLE

Mr Mashego has the Impairment Plus benefit with
a Whole of Life cover period and a sum assured of
R1 000 000. At age 75 Mr Mashego suffers a brain
injury and starts to suffer from receptive aphasia and is
partially unable to comprehend language symbols. This
condition qualifies Mr Mashego for the 0.5% monthly
benefit level. This means that Mr Mashego will be paid
R5 000 per month (i.e. R1 000 000 x 0.5%) increasing
by inflation every year for the remainder of his life.
If Mr Mashego’s aphasia condition had been more
serious and he was totally unable to comprehend
language symbols he would have qualified for the
1.5% monthly benefit level. In this case his initial claim
amount would be R15 000 rather than the R5 000
described above.

Whole of Life cover period: What happens if I suffer an
impairment and then either my condition worsens or I
suffer a second impairment?
If you have already made a partial claim on your benefit, you
will still receive an additional claim payment (or an increased
claim payment) if either your existing impairment worsens or
if you suffer another unrelated impairment. The tables that
follow show how your additional or increased claim amount
will be calculated in the various possible scenarios.

Mr Smith has the Impairment Plus benefit with
the Whole of Life cover period and a sum assured
of R1 000 000. At age 34 Mr Smith is involved in an
accident at work and loses his foot. He is permanently
impaired. This qualifies for a 25% pay-out, and he is
paid R250 000 (25% of R1 000 000). At age 35, Mr
Smith is involved in a car accident and loses his left
arm below his elbow, again this condition renders him
to be permanently impaired. This qualifies for a 50%
pay-out under The Limbs Benefit Category. Since he
has already been paid 25% for that Benefit Category,
he will receive 25% of his sum assured. Therefore, he
will be paid R250 000 (i.e., 25% of R1 000 000).
Suppose that many years later, at age 74, Mr
Smith suffers a heart attack and is diagnosed with
permanent coronary artery disease. Since Mr Smith
is considered retired, he will be assessed under the
post-retirement phase of the benefit. The severity of
his condition qualifies him for 0.5% of his sum assured
as a monthly benefit amount. Mr Smith has already
received 50% of his sum assured in pre-retirement
claims, his post-retirement claim will therefore be
based on the remaining 50% of his sum assured. He
will be paid R2 650 per month. This is calculated as R
1 000 000 X 50% = R 500 000. The sum of R 500
000 is then multiplied by 0.5% to give a monthly
amount of R 2500. A post-retirement claim has been
admitted, so premiums are no longer required to be
paid for the benefit.
After every claim anniversary Mr Smith’s claim amount
will be increased by CPI (capped at 10.5%). If CPI is
10% his claim amount will be increased to R2 750 at
the first claim anniversary, R3 025 after the second
claim anniversary. Thereafter, the annual escalation will
continue to be applied at every claim anniversary.
Soon after the second claim anniversary Mr Smith is
diagnosed as suffering from severe aphasia and meets
the criteria for the 1.5% per month pay-out level.
Mr Smith’s benefit of R3 025 will be increased by a
factor of 3. Considering that the 1.5% pay-out level he
qualifies for now is 3 times the 0.5% pay-out level he
was previously receiving. Mr Smith’s monthly claim
amount therefore increases to R9 075.

BENEFITS | LOSS OF INCOME PROTECTION PILLAR: LUMP SUM | OCTOBER 2025

LIFESTYLE PROTECTOR TECHNICAL GUIDE

3. The digestive system
3.1 Upper gastrointestinal tract
This benefit category covers documented, ongoing, organic diseases of the mouth, oesophagus, stomach, pancreas or small
bowel. Constitutional symptoms including inappropriate fatigue, sustained weight loss and signs due to malabsorption (e.g.
anaemia) must be present.

CRITERIA FOR PAYMENT

• Moderate to severe
exacerbations with severe pain;
and
• Symptoms and signs not
completely controlled by
medical or surgical treatment;
and
• Weight loss resulting in a BMI
of less than 16 due to disease,
irrespective of weight prior to
effects of disease.

7. The endocrine system
This benefit category covers diseases and disorders affecting the endocrine system. Diseases and conditions covered include:
•

Hypothalamic/pituitary axis diseases;

•

Hypoadrenalism;

•

Hyperadrenocorticism;

•

Phaeochromocytoma; and

•

Type 1 or 2 diabetes mellitus.

These diseases and conditions must be supported by documented medical evidence.

CRITERIA FOR PAYMENT

• Symptoms and signs of
abnormality of the endocrine
system leading to target
end-organ damage despite
appropriate surgical and medical
treatment including replacement
or blockades

9. The visual system
This benefit category covers diseases and trauma to the eye(s) and supporting structures and is measured using the best
corrected vision only.
In the event of trauma to the eye(s), this benefit only covers complications or conditions that are specified below and
that arise within 24 months of the occurrence of the traumatic event. The traumatic event must have occurred after the
commencement of the benefit and is caused by an external physical force, for example a fall or motor vehicle accident.
Claims must be confirmed by an appropriate registered specialist together with any relevant supporting and appropriate
documents such as hospital, police or accident reports in the case of a traumatic event.

12. The spine
This benefit category covers injury or disease of the spinal column only.

15. Activities of Daily Living Catch-All
This benefit category covers all diseases, disorders or injuries
which result in a permanent functional impairment but is
not covered in the Impairment Benefit Categories above.
Impairment is measured by assessing the ability of the Life
Assured to perform Basic and Instrumental activities of daily
living (ADLs) specified below.
The ability to perform an activity will be assessed as being
able to perform it independently with assistive devices and/
or equipment where indicated and without supervision.

CRITERIA FOR PAYMENT

BASIC ADLS
Washing

The ability to wash oneself without physical assistance or supervision. This includes transferring in
and out of the bath or shower.

Dressing

The ability to independently put on or take off all garments, including the securing and unfastening
thereof. Where appropriate, this includes any braces, prosthesis or other surgical appliance.

Feeding / eating

The ability to eat independently once food has been prepared and made available.

Continence

The ability to manage bowel and bladder functions such that an adequate level of personal hygiene
can be maintained.

Mobility

The ability to move independently between indoor rooms on a level surface with the assistance of a
walking aid, including a wheelchair.

Transferring

The ability to independently transfer from the bed to a chair with the assistance of a walking aid.
INSTRUMENTAL ADLS

Telephone use
(communication)

The ability to use a telephone independently. This includes answering the phone on an incoming call,
being able to hold a basic conversation, as well as the ability to dial a well-known or written down
number.

Food preparation

The ability to prepare and serve simple, everyday meals. This includes the ability to perform simple
measurements, preparation activities and having awareness of general kitchen safety.

Housekeeping

The ability to perform light household chores such as making a bed, washing dishes and maintaining a
reasonable level of cleanliness.

Transport

The ability to safely drive a car (including getting into and out of the car) or the ability to use public
transport, including being able to provide a drop-off address, and reaching a public transport pick-up
point.

Handling finances

The ability to perform basic transactions such as purchasing daily consumables, including the ability to
perform a basic calculation of what money is required to pay for purchases and what change is due.

Writing

The ability to take down a simple message or complete a simple form requiring personal details such
as name, address and date of birth.

BENEFITS | LOSS OF INCOME PROTECTION PILLAR: LUMP SUM | OCTOBER 2025

LIFESTYLE PROTECTOR TECHNICAL GUIDE

ABSOLUTE PROTECTOR
Benefit Description

Absolute Protector pays you a lump sum if you are impaired or if you are unable to perform your
occupational duties. The amount of the benefit depends on whether you are occupationally disabled,
and on the severity of your impairment.
If you have selected either a Lifestyle Protector (Term) policy or a Lifestyle Protector (Term with
PayOut) policy, only Absolute Protector (OOD) can be selected.
If you have selected the Whole of Life cover period, once you are retired the nature of your cover will
change. After retirement, if you become permanently impaired as defined in the policy document,
you will receive a monthly benefit which increases each year with inflation (as measured by CPI
capped at 10.5%) for the remainder of your life. The amount of your monthly benefit is a proportion of
the sum assured and depends on the severity of your impairment.
Post-retirement Term benefits will only be admitted on permanent impairment, where the amount
payable will be dependent on the severity specified in the Impairment Benefit Categories grid.

Benefit Type

This is an accelerated benefit that can be added to either or both of the Life Cover and the
Renewable Life Cover(1) benefits on the policy.
If you have selected the term benefit, it will reduce the total of your Life Cover (Term) sum assured
by the amount of any claim made. Your other accelerated benefits may also be affected by claiming
since the maximum accelerated benefit cover allowed is restricted to the amount of your Life Cover
(Term) sum assured.
If you have selected the To end of the month of the Life Assured’s: 65th or 70th birthday cover
period, or if you have selected the Whole of Life cover period and claim before retirement, this benefit
reduces the total of your Life Cover and Renewable Life Cover(1) by the amount of the claim made.
Your other accelerated benefits may also be affected by claiming since the maximum accelerated
benefit cover allowed is restricted to the amount of your Life Cover plus your Renewable Life
Cover(1).
If you have selected the Whole of Life cover period, any claims for this benefit after retirement will not
reduce your Life Cover or Renewable Life Cover(1) even though it is an accelerated benefit.

Cover period

• Whole of Life
• To end of the month of the Life Assured’s: 65th and 70th birthday
• Term: Minimum - 5 years.
Maximum - Lesser of 70 minus age next birthday and 25 years.

Benefit Limits

Minimum: R100 000
Maximum: 	Term cover, To end of the month of the Life Assured’s: 65th and 70th birthday: R60
million (aggregated over all Loss of Income Protection benefits)
	Whole of Life cover period: R9 million (aggregated over all Loss of Income Protection
benefits)
	If the Whole of Life cover period is selected on both the Income Protector benefit
and Absolute Protector benefit, the sum of 1.5% of the Absolute Protector sum
assured and 50% of the Income Protector sum assured cannot exceed 55% of after-tax
insurable monthly income.

Premium Pattern

• Level Premium Pattern
• Renewable Premium Pattern(2)
• Age Rated Premium Pattern
• Fixed Increasing Premium Pattern
Term: Only Level Premium Pattern available on term benefits.

BENEFITS | LOSS OF INCOME PROTECTION PILLAR: LUMP SUM | OCTOBER 2025

LIFESTYLE PROTECTOR TECHNICAL GUIDE

What is the Absolute Protector benefit?

Additional cover provided by the Whole of Life term

Absolute Protector provides you with comprehensive
disability and impairment cover. It offers the best of both the
Capital Disability and the Impairment benefits by paying
a lump sum benefit on either your inability to do your job
or on loss of your body functionality, or both, after an injury
or illness. All claims will therefore be assessed to determine
whether you suffer from a functional impairment as well
as your ability to perform your occupational duties. Your
benefits are tiered based on the level of impairment suffered.

After retirement you no longer face the risk of being unable
to work if you become permanently occupationally disabled
or impaired, however you do face the risk that you become
permanently impaired and this creates unforeseen expenses
which effectively reduce your income after retirement. If you
have selected the Whole of Life cover period option you will
also be covered against this risk in addition to the normal
cover before retirement. If the Whole of Life cover period
has been chosen, and you become permanently impaired
(as defined in the policy document) after retirement, the
Absolute Protector benefit will pay a monthly benefit
amount which increases each year with inflation (as
measured by CPI capped at 10.5%). The monthly payments
will continue for the remainder of your life. The amount of
your monthly benefit will be a proportion of your benefit
amount and depends on the severity of the impairment.

This is an accelerated benefit that can be added to either
or both of the Life Cover and Renewable Life Cover(1)
benefits. This benefit reduces the total of your Life Cover
and Renewable Life Cover(1) benefits by the amount of any
claim. Your other accelerated benefits may also be affected
by claiming since the maximum accelerated benefit cover
allowed is restricted to the amount of your Life Cover plus
your Renewable Life Cover(1).

Even though Absolute Protector is an accelerated benefit,
any monthly benefit payments made under the postretirement phase of the benefit will not reduce Life Cover
and Renewable Life Cover(1) benefits.

BEFORE RETIREMENT

AFTER RETIREMENT

Your need:
Protection against the risk that you will not be able to earn an
income due to a disability or an impairment.

Your need:
Protection against the risk that an impairment in old age leads to
expenses which effectively reduce your retirement income.

Conditions for payment:
You are unable to work due to a disability or you suffer an
impairment before retirement, as defined in the policy document.

Conditions for payment:
You suffer an impairment after retirement, as defined, in the policy
document.

Type of payment:
A lump sum amount which will
reduce your Life Cover and/or
Renewable Life Cover(1).

Type of payment:
A monthly benefit payment, which increases by CPI each year for
the rest of your life. The monthly benefit payments will not reduce
your Life Cover or Renewable
Life Cover(1) benefits. The amount of the
benefit depends on the severity of
the impairment suffered.

Before Retirement
(1)

The Renewable Life Cover option is closed to New Business from July 2022.

(2)

The Renewable Premium Pattern is closed to New Business from July 2022.

Retirement

After Retirement

Death

BENEFITS | LOSS OF INCOME PROTECTION PILLAR: LUMP SUM | OCTOBER 2025

EXAMPLE

LIFESTYLE PROTECTOR TECHNICAL GUIDE

•

Mr Mashego has the Absolute Protector (OOD)
benefit with the Whole of Life cover period and a sum
assured of R1 000 000 as well as Life Cover with a sum
assured of R1 000 000.
Claim before retirement
If Mr Mashego is in a car accident at age 35 (i.e., before
retirement), he suffers severe injuries to his head, which
causes permanent and worsening cognitive symptoms.
His symptoms renders him unable to permanently
perform his own occupational duties, his Absolute
Protector benefit will pay out R1 000 000. His Life
Cover benefit amount will reduce by R1 000 000 to
zero and end.
Suppose Mr Mashego was in the same car accident,
and he lost his leg below the knee. This does not hinder
him from performing his occupational duties, as an
accountant. The claim will not be valid for a pay-out
under the occupational disability requirement. Mr
Mashego’s claim will, however, be considered for a 50%
pay-out under the permanent Impairment benefit
category, The Limbs. He will receive a pay-out of R700
000 (i.e., 50% of R1 000 000). His Life Cover sum
assured will reduce by R700 000.
Claim after retirement
If Mr Mashego’s car accident had occurred at age 75,
the claim would be assessed under the post-retirement
cover phase of the benefit. As a result of the accident,
Mr Mashego is permanently paralysed. Paralysis
qualifies for a monthly payment of 1.5% of the benefit
amount. He will therefore be paid R15 000 per month
(R1 000 000 x 1.5%) and will be paid for the rest of
his life. These monthly pay-outs will help to protect Mr
Mashego’s retirement income from being eroded by
additional medical expenses. For example, he may need
to employ a nurse on a full- or part-time basis to help
care for him.
These post-retirement monthly pay-outs will not reduce
his Life Cover benefit amount and his full R1 000 000
cover will continue.
In addition to the monthly benefit, Mr Mashego will
no longer have to pay the premiums for the Absolute
Protector benefit. Mr Mashego’s benefit pay-outs will
also increase annually in line with inflation. Assuming
inflation is 10%, his monthly claim pay-out would increase
to R16 500 in the second year of claim, R18 150 in the
third year, and continue to increase every claim year
thereafter.

What type of cover does the Absolute Protector benefit
provide?
Absolute Protector provides both impairment and
occupational disability cover. There are two types of
disability cover that you can choose under this benefit:

Own occupation (OOD)
The benefit is payable if you cannot do your specific job.
For example, if you are a surgeon and suffer from an
injury, then this benefit will be paid even if you have the
experience and training to become a lecturer.
Note that when Absolute Protector is selected as a
term benefit, only OOD will be available.

•

Own or any reasonable occupation (OD)
The benefit will only be paid if you are unable to perform
your job or any reasonable job. So, using the example
above, if the surgeon has the experience and training to
be a lecturer, but is unable to perform this role because
of the disability, then the claim will be paid.

100% of the sum assured will be paid in the event of you
being occupationally disabled. The conditions covered in the
event of impairment are the same as under the Impairment
benefit. The percentage of the sum assured payable
depends on the level of impairment suffered.
How do benefit increases affect the tiered claim
payments?
The amount of any claim is based on:
•

The sum assured at the date of claim

•

The percentage of the sum assured payable as a
result of the impairment/disability, and any claims
previously paid.
EXAMPLE

Mr Smith has Life Cover and Absolute Protector
with a R1 000 000 sum assured and a 7% automatic
benefit increase. He loses his hand at the MP joint in
an accident 6 months after the inception of the policy.
This does not render him permanently occupationally
disabled, rather he is permanently impaired, therefore
a 50% benefit ( R700 000) is paid out under The
Limbs benefit category. His Life Cover and Absolute
Protector benefits are reduced to R700 000. At the
end of the year the remaining sum assured increases
by 7% to R535 000. He is diagnosed with terminal
Cancer 17 months after the inception of the policy and
is assessed as occupationally disabled. The full benefit
of R535 000 is paid and both the Life Cover and
Absolute Protector benefits cease.

After I suffer an impairment and claim, is there a waiting
period before I can receive my benefit?
No. Your claim will be paid as soon as Liberty has established
the permanence of the condition you are claiming for.

BENEFITS | LOSS OF INCOME PROTECTION PILLAR: LUMP SUM | OCTOBER 2025

What impact does my occupation have on my benefit?
Your occupation has a direct bearing on your ability to claim
and hence your eventual benefit.
EXAMPLE

Mr Jones is a bank manager. He has an accident and
loses his dominant hand. While the injury would impair
his ability to play golf, this injury might not permanently
affect his ability to perform his occupational
duties. Should Mr Jones still be able to perform his
occupational duties, he would not be able to claim for
occupational disability. However, he would be able to
claim for his permanent impairment, and 50% of the
sum assured would be paid, under The Limbs benefit
category. However, if Mr Jones was a surgeon, this
would seriously affect his ability to perform surgery.
In this case, he suffers both permanent occupational
disability and permanent impairment. Liberty would
pay the maximum benefit (100%) of Mr Jones’s claim in
this instance for occupational disability.

LIFESTYLE PROTECTOR TECHNICAL GUIDE

Whole of Life cover period: What will my monthly claim
amount be if I suffer an impairment after retirement?
Your claim amount will be equal to 0.5%, 1% or 1.5% of your
benefit amount depending on the type of impairment you
suffer and the severity of the impairment.
Your benefit amount will increase at each claim anniversary
by inflation (as measured by CPI and capped at 10.5% per
annum). More information on the levels of payout can
be found in the Impairment Benefit Categories section.
However, you should always consult your policy document
for the claim percentages applicable to your policy.
EXAMPLE

Mr Mashego has the Absolute Protector benefit with a
Whole of Life cover period and a sum assured of R1 000
000. At age 75 Mr Mashego suffers a brain injury and
starts to suffer from permanent receptive aphasia and is
partially unable to comprehend language symbols. This
condition qualifies Mr Mashego for the 0.5% monthly
benefit level. This means that Mr Mashego will be paid
R5 000 per month (i.e. R1 000 000 x 0.5%) increasing
by inflation every year for the remainder of his life.

Whole of Life cover period: Why does the benefit
provide a lump sum before retirement, but a monthly
income benefit after retirement?
The Absolute Protector benefit meets different needs
before and after retirement. Before retirement the benefit
protects you against the risk that you are unable to earn an
income due to a disability or impairment. Usually people will
have some form of debt before retirement, for example a
house or car loan. A lump sum payment can help settle that
debt immediately should you suffer a disability or impairment
which prevents you from being able to earn an income. After
retirement, you no longer face the risk of not being able
to work but you do face the risk that you suffer a serious
impairment which leads to additional, unforeseen expenses
such as the cost of a nurse to care for you, or a driver to
transport you. These types of expenses will effectively reduce
your retirement income. Since you are not likely to have any
debt after retirement and that your new expenses will be
incurred predominantly on a monthly basis, a monthly benefit
amount more closely meets your need for protection.
Can I select the Whole of Life cover period on both the
Absolute Protector benefit and the Income Protector
benefit?
Yes, you may select the Whole of Life cover period on both
the Income Protector benefit and the Absolute Protector
benefit, provided that:
• 1.5% of the Absolute Protector sum assured; plus
•

50% of the Income Protector sum assured

do not exceed 55% of your after-tax insurable monthly
income.
Whole of Life cover period: When will I be considered
retired for this benefit?
When assessing claims for this benefit you will be considered
retired:
• At the policy anniversary immediately preceding your
70th birthday; or
• If you are over the age of 55 and you have stopped
active or full-time engagement in your occupation (but
not due to the impairment that you are claiming for).

If Mr Mashego’s aphasia condition had been more
serious and he was totally unable to comprehend
language symbols he would have qualified for the 1.5%
monthly benefit level. In this case his initial claim amount
would be R15 000 per month rather than the R5 000
per month described above.
Are there restrictions as to which Loss of Income
Protection benefits I can select on my policy?
You may select only one of the lump sum Loss of Income
Protection benefits on your policy.
On a Lifestyle Protector policy you can choose either:
• Capital Disability, or
• Capital Disability Plus, or
• Impairment, or
• Impairment Plus, or
• Absolute Protector, or
• Absolute Protector Plus, or
• Absolute Impairment, or
• Absolute Impairment Plus, or
• Vital Living, or
•

Vital Living Plus.

On a Lifestyle Protector (Term) or Lifestyle Protector (Term
with PayOut) policy you can choose either:
• Capital Disability, or
• Capital Disability Plus, or
• Absolute Protector, or
• Absolute Protector Plus, or
• Absolute Impairment, or
• Absolute Impairment Plus.
Your Second Life Assured may choose a different lump sum
Loss of Income Protection benefit to you. Income Range
benefits can be selected even if you already have a lump
sum Loss of Income Protection benefit on your Lifestyle
Protector policy.
